## Level 1 · M01 Starting Work · 1.1 Understanding What "Professional" Means at Work (`CE-L01-M01-L01`)

**#1 — Story continuity**
- Original: “Chowdhury:”
- Replacement: “Sadia:”
- Reason: Speaker label used her surname while she introduces herself as Sadia and the exercises call her Sadia.

**#2 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “Mixing the two up is MIS-0002 .”
- Replacement: “Using one form when you mean the other is MIS-0002 .”
- Reason: Aligns with the corrected MIS-0002 explanation (the issue is unclear meaning, not a forbidden sentence).

**#3 — Production language / unfinished material**
- Original: “-- no dedicated Grammar-in-Context card for this lesson; the pattern is light enough to model directly inside the dialogue and practice.”
- Replacement: “. You will see it throughout the dialogue and the practice.”
- Reason: Internal production note about curriculum cards.

**#4 — Grammar / usage explanation** · source: QA review of the reference PDF p.14; Cambridge Dictionary, 'professional' (adjective, noun)
- Original: “❌ I want to become professional. ✅ I want to become more professional. / I want to become a professional in this field. "Professional" works as an adjective (more professional, be professional) or as a noun with an article (a professional). Dropping the article or the comparative leaves the sentence ambiguous about which sense is meant.”
- Replacement: “⚠️ Unclear: I want to become professional. ✅ About behaviour: I want to become more professional. / I want to be more professional at work. ✅ About a person with skills or training: I want to become a professional in this field. "I want to become professional" is not ungrammatical, but at work it is unclear. Professional is an adjective when it describes behaviour ( be professional , more profess…”
- Reason: The original marked the sentence as simply wrong. It is grammatical; the real problem is ambiguity between the adjective and noun meanings.

**#5 — Pronunciation / word stress** · source: QA review of the reference PDF p.14; Cambridge Dictionary pronunciation
- Original: “Word stress: proFESsional, PUNCtual, resPECTful (stress falls on the second syllable in all three).”
- Replacement: “Word stress: proFESsional and resPECTful are stressed on the second syllable; PUNCtual is stressed on the first.”
- Reason: 'Punctual' is stressed on the first syllable (/ˈpʌŋk.tʃu.əl/), so the claim that all three have second-syllable stress was wrong.

**#6 — Audio availability labelling**
- Original: “Listen to the dialogue. What three things does Sadia say”
- Replacement: “Read the dialogue again (or listen to a partner or teacher read it aloud). What three things does Sadia say”
- Reason: No audio recording exists yet; the instruction now works without one.

**#7 — Exercise and answer key alignment** · source: QA review of the reference PDF pp.16–17
- Original: “The lesson's three core actions are punctuality, listening, and respect.”
- Replacement: “respectful — "Being professional means being on time, listening carefully, and being respectful." The blank needs an adjective after "being", parallel to the lesson's three actions (punctuality, listening, respect).”
- Reason: The answer key gave the noun 'respect' (and a summary) rather than the required word 'respectful'.

**#8 — Production language / unfinished material**
- Original: “4. Open-ended -- check against the lesson's model language.”
- Replacement: “4. Answers will vary. Sample answer: "For me, being professional means being on time and ready to work, listening carefully when someone explains something, and speaking to everyone with respect. It also means keeping my promises, even small ones." A good answer names concrete actions, not just "being good at the job".”
- Reason: Unfinished model answer replaced with a sample answer and success criteria.

## Level 1 · M01 Starting Work · 1.3 Introducing Yourself Professionally (`CE-L01-M01-L03`)

**#17 — Reference codes and links**

- Replacement: “There is no new grammar in this lesson. It combines the professional language of Lesson 1.1 and the background phrasing of Lesson 1.2 (past simple / present perfect) into one introduction sequence.”
- Reason: Internal lesson codes (L01, L02, L03) replaced with the book's lesson numbers.

**#18 — Natural professional English**
- Original: “The person you take instructions from and give updates to”
- Replacement: “To have someone as your direct boss: you take instructions from them and give them updates”
- Reason: The definition described a person, but the entry is a verb phrase.

**#19 — Pronunciation / word stress** · source: Cambridge Dictionary
- Original: “Word stress: TEAM, dePARTment, rePORT (verb, second syllable stressed).”
- Replacement: “Word stress: dePARTment, rePORT (second syllable, for both the verb and the noun).”
- Reason: 'Team' has one syllable, so it has no stress pattern to learn; 'report' is stressed on the second syllable as a noun too, not only as a verb.

**#20 — Bengali / Hindi support**
- Original: “There isn't a separate Bengali grammar point in this lesson -- it's about switching register , not tense or structure. The formula stays the same in both languages; what shifts is politeness markers (e.g. addressing someone respectfully) rather than sentence structure.”
- Replacement: “In Bengali, respect is shown partly through the pronoun: আপনি for a senior person, তুমি for a peer. English has only one "you", so the register shift has to come from other places: the greeting ("Good morning" rather than "Hi"), full forms ("my name is" rather than "I'm"), and a slightly slower pace. The content of the introduction stays the same.”
- Reason: Replaced a note saying there was nothing to note with a specific, accurate contrast that explains where English carries formality.

**#21 — Bengali / Hindi support**
- Original: “Same principle in Hindi -- the content of an introduction doesn't change between a peer and a senior person, but respectful address and a slightly more measured pace signal the formality shift, just as in English.”
- Replacement: “Hindi marks respect with आप (formal) versus तुम (informal). English "you" is the same for everyone, so a learner cannot rely on the pronoun: the greeting, full forms and pace carry the formality instead. Do not add "sir" or "ma'am" to every sentence to make up for it; one respectful greeting is enough.”
- Reason: Made the Hindi note specific and accurate (आप/तुम versus the single English 'you').

**#22 — Audio availability labelling**
- Original: “Listen to both scenes. What team does Arif say he will be part of?”
- Replacement: “Read both scenes again. What team does Arif say he will be part of?”
- Reason: No audio recording exists yet.

- Replacement: “Model answer: "Hi, I'm Arif. I studied hospitality management and worked at a small hotel for about a year. I'm looking forward to being part of the team." Keep one sentence for each part (name, background/role, closing line) and remove hesitations and side stories (MIS-0004).”
- Reason: The answer key restated the principle but gave no rewritten introduction.

- Reason: The sections said no support was needed; embedded-question word order is a real and common difficulty for these learners.

## Level 1 · M06 Basic Workplace Vocabulary · 6.1 People, Roles and Departments (`CE-L01-M06-L01`)

- Replacement: “Priyanka works on the front desk.”
- Reason: Priyanka is Arif's front-office peer throughout the book.

**#156 — Grammar / usage explanation** · source: Cambridge Dictionary, 'reception' (UK: at reception)
- Original: “specific staffed counter itself — you'd say "leave it at the front desk," not usually "leave it at the reception." In many workplaces the words are used almost interchangeably, but front desk is the more precise choice when you mean the counter itself.”
- Replacement: “specific staffed counter itself. Both are correct, but they behave differently: reception is normally used without "the" after at ("Please wait at reception"), while front desk always takes "the" ("Leave it at the front desk"). "Leave it at the reception" is the mistake to avoid. In hotels, front desk is the usual name for the counter and for the team that works there.”
- Reason: The original implied that 'leave it at reception' was unusual. 'At reception' (no article) is standard; the real error is adding 'the'.

**#157 — Exercise and answer key alignment**
- Original: “A colleague says: "Ask the reception if you need help with that." Rewrite this using the more precise word for the staffed counter itself, and explain the difference in one sentence.”
- Replacement: “A colleague says: "Ask the reception if you need help with that." Correct the sentence in two ways: once with "reception" and once with "front desk".”
- Reason: The exercise now tests the article point the corrected explanation teaches.

**#158 — Exercise and answer key alignment**
- Original: “Suggested answer: Ask the front desk if you need help with that. ("Reception" is the general area/function; "front desk" is the specific staffed counter.)”
- Replacement: “"Ask at reception if you need help with that." (no "the") / "Ask the front desk if you need help with that."”
- Reason: Aligned with the corrected explanation.

## Level 1 · — · Assessment Level 1 Assessment (`CE-L01-ASSESSMENT`)

**#182 — Assessment instructions and scoring**
- Original: “/<h2>Part A -- Listening comprehension</h2>.*?</table>/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “<h2>Part A — Reading: choose the best response (items 1–7)</h2> <p>Read each short workplace situation and choose the most appropriate response. There is one item per module, in order. (Audio is not available yet, so this part is read, not heard.)</p> <h2>Part B — Speaking (items 8–14)</h2> <p>Respond aloud to each prompt, and record yourself if you can. There is no single correct wording: each response is scored with the criteria below, and a sample answer is given in the key so you can compare.</p> <h2>Part C — Writing (items 15–18)</h2> <p>Write a short response to each scenario. Most combine two modules' language, the same way real situations rarely stay inside one lesson.</p> <h2>Scoring</h2> <p><strong>Part A:</strong> 1 point for each correct answer (7 points).</p> <p><strong>Parts B and C:</strong> score each item from 0 to 3 — one point for each criterion it meets. Only criteria that apply to that kind of task are used:</p> <table> <thead> <tr><th>Criterion (1 point)</th><th>Part B — speaking</th><th>Part C — writing</th></tr> </thead> <tbody> <tr><td>Task</td><td>Does exactly what the prompt asks (for example, names both options, states the purpose, restates the deadline).</td><td>Same.</td></tr> <tr><td>Language</td><td>Uses the module's workplace phrases and vocabulary accurately.</td><td>Same, with correct grammar and spelling.</td></tr> <tr><td>Tone and delivery</td><td>Register fits the listener; clear and easy to understand, without long hesitation or repeated apologies.</td><td>Register fits the reader; polite, clear and appropriately brief.</td></tr> </tbody> </table> <p>Maximum: Part A 7 + Part B 21 + Part C 12 = <strong>40 points</strong>.</p>”
- Reason: The rubric applied the same weighted criteria to spoken and written tasks (pronunciation and 'delivery' cannot be scored in writing), referred to 'Module 2's pilot assessment', and never explained how to turn a score into a result. Part A was called 'listening' although it is read. Replaced with criteria that apply to each part and a points total.

## Level 2 · M01 Daily Work Updates · 1.1 Giving a Clear Status Update (`CE-L02-M01-L01`)

- Replacement: “(removed)”
- Reason: Moved into Arif's main update (see previous correction).

**#211 — Grammar / usage explanation** · source: Cambridge Grammar of English, present perfect (current relevance); BrE/AmE usage
- Original: “Bengali/Hindi often uses one simple past-tense-like form for both ideas, which is why English learners sometimes say "I finished" when "I've finished" fits the moment better here: the finished task still matters to what happens next, which is exactly what the present perfect signals.”
- Replacement: “Bengali and Hindi speakers often use a simple past form where English prefers the present perfect, so they say "I finished the checklist" when "I've finished the checklist" fits a status update better: the finished task still matters to what happens next, which is exactly what the present perfect signals. ("I just finished…" is also common, especially in American English.)”
- Reason: The original said the L1s use 'one form for both ideas' without saying which ideas; the contrast is now stated precisely, with the American English note.

## Level 2 · M02 Making & Responding to Requests · 2.1 Requests with Conditions and Trade-offs (`CE-L02-M02-L01`)

**#216 — Natural professional English**
- Original: “A condition on your side: "Could you cover the desk for twenty minutes, if you get a chance?" -- leaves room for a genuine no.”
- Replacement: “An open condition: "Could you cover the desk for twenty minutes, if you get a chance?" — leaves room for a genuine no.”
- Reason: Both examples are conditions about the listener's situation; the real contrast is open ('if you get a chance') versus specific ('as long as it doesn't clash with your break').

**#217 — Natural professional English**
- Original: “A condition on their side: "As long as it doesn't clash with your break, could you cover the desk for twenty minutes?"”
- Replacement: “A specific condition: "As long as it doesn't clash with your break, could you cover the desk for twenty minutes?"”
- Reason: See previous correction.


## Level 2 · M02 Making & Responding to Requests · 2.4 Relaying a Request Between People (`CE-L02-M02-L04`)

**#218 — Grammar / usage explanation** · source: QA review of the reference PDF (reported speech, p.480); Cambridge Grammar of English §§ reported speech, backshift
- Original: “When you relay what someone said, the tense usually shifts back one step:”
- Replacement: “Whether the tense changes depends on two things: The reporting verb. With a present reporting verb ( is asking , wants to know , says ), the tense does not change: "He wants to know if you're free this afternoon." Whether it is still true. With a past reporting verb ( said , asked ), the tense usually moves back one step ("She said she could help"). But if the information is still true or still i…”
- Reason: The lesson said the tense 'usually shifts back', but its own table and dialogue use present reporting verbs with no shift. Backshift depends on the reporting verb and is optional when the reported fact is still true.

**#219 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “She said she could help.”
- Replacement: “She said she could help. (past reporting verb: tense moves back)”
- Reason: Each table row now shows why the tense does or does not change.

**#220 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “He wants to know if you're free this afternoon.”
- Replacement: “He wants to know if you're free this afternoon. (present reporting verb: no change)”
- Reason: See previous correction.

**#221 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “She said she'd get back to you.”
- Replacement: “She said she'd get back to you. / She said she'll get back to you. (both correct: the promise is still in the future)”
- Reason: See previous correction.

**#222 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “English reported speech is stricter -- the tense and pronoun shift is what tells the listener”
- Replacement: “In English, the pronoun shift (and, after a past reporting verb, often a tense shift) is what tells the listener”
- Reason: Aligned with the corrected explanation: the pronoun shift is required, the tense shift is not always.

- Replacement: “Sample: "The guest in Room 215 is asking if she can get a late checkout." (Also correct: "…asked if she could get a late checkout.")”

## Level 2 · M05 Apologizing & Thanking Professionally · 5.1 A Real Apology vs. a Weak One (`CE-L02-M05-L01`)

**#244 — Natural professional English**
- Original: “I want to acknowledge that forgetting to pass that message on caused real confusion for the guest. That's on me -- I should have flagged it the moment I took it. Going forward, I'll write every message down immediately, so this doesn't happen again.”
- Replacement: “I'm sorry, Ms. Noor — I forgot to pass that message on, and the guest was left waiting for a callback. That's on me — I should have flagged it the moment I took it. Going forward, I'll write every message down immediately, so this doesn't happen again.”
- Reason: 'I want to acknowledge that…' is the language of a formal written statement; spoken to a supervisor, a natural apology starts with 'I'm sorry' and then names the impact.

**#245 — Natural professional English**
- Original: “message the moment I take it, so this doesn't happen again."”
- Replacement: “message the moment I take it, so this doesn't happen again." In speech, it is natural to start with "I'm sorry" and then acknowledge the impact ("I'm sorry — I forgot to pass that message on, and…"). "I want to acknowledge that…" is more typical of a formal written apology.”
- Reason: Added the register note that makes the spoken model and the written-style example consistent.

**#285 — Assessment instructions and scoring**

There is no single correct wording: each response is scored with the criteria below, and a sample answer is given in the key so you can compare. One prompt per module, Module 1 through Module 8.</p> <h2>Part C — Writing (items 17–20)</h2> <p>Write a short response to each of four scenarios. Each scenario combines two modules' language at once, the same way a real situation rarely announces which lesson it belongs to:</p> <ul> <li>Daily Updates + Following Up</li> <li>Requests + Apologizing/Thanking</li> <li>Telephone + Digital Messaging</li> <li>Customer Interaction + Workplace Etiquette</li> </ul> <h2>Scoring</h2> <p><strong>Part A:</strong> 1 point for each correct answer (8 points).</p> <p><strong>Parts B and C:</strong> score each item from 0 to 3 — one point for each criterion it meets. ## Level 3 · M07 Professional Grammar in Context · 7.1 Tenses and Aspect for Professional Writing (`CE-L03-M07-L01`)

**#365 — Grammar / usage explanation** · source: QA review of the reference PDF pp.470–474
- Original: “his tenses keep sliding between "this already happened and is finished" and "this is still going on right now."”
- Replacement: “his tenses keep sliding between "this happened at a finished time in the past" and "this is connected to now."”
- Reason: The lesson reduced the present perfect to 'finished versus still going on'. The present perfect very often describes completed actions ("I've sent the invoice"); what decides the tense is whether the time is finished and stated (past simple) or the action is connected to now — a present result, an unstated time, or a period up to now (present perfect).

**#366 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “/(<h2>Key Workplace Lesson</h2>\s*).*?(?=<div class=\"dialogue-box\">)/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1<p>Professional writing almost always needs to answer one quiet question for the reader: is this part of a finished past, or is it connected to now? English answers that with two forms, and mixing them up is one of the most common accuracy problems in workplace writing: the <strong>past simple</strong> and the <strong>present perfect</strong>.</p> <p>The <strong>past simple</strong> (<em>we completed, I sent, the team finished</em>) places an action in a finished time: it happened then, and that time is over — usually with a time expression that says or implies when (<em>yesterday, on Monday, last week, in Q2</em>).</p> <p>The <strong>present perfect</strong> (<em>we have completed, I have sent, the team has finished</em>) connects the past to now. It is <em>not</em> only for things that are still going on — it is very often used for actions that are complete:</p> <ul> <li>a completed action whose result matters now: "I've sent the invoice" (so it's done — you don't need to chase me);</li> <li>an action at an unstated time up to now: "We've completed three audits this year";</li> <li>a situation or series that continues up to now: "We've completed 60% of the follow-up items so far."</li> </ul> <p>So the key question is not "finished or unfinished?" but "finished <em>time</em>, or connected to now?" The two forms aren't interchangeable, and a careful reader notices when they're swapped.</p> ”
- Reason: The lesson reduced the present perfect to 'finished versus still going on'. The present perfect very often describes completed actions ("I've sent the invoice"); what decides the tense is whether the time is finished and stated (past simple) or the action is connected to now — a present result, an unstated time, or a period up to now (present perfect).

**#367 — Grammar / usage explanation** · source: QA review of the reference PDF pp.470–474
- Original: “And the follow-up items are still ongoing right now, so that part needs present perfect. Try it again.”
- Replacement: “And the 60% is a result up to now — "so far", the work isn't finished — so that part needs present perfect. Try it again.”
- Reason: The lesson reduced the present perfect to 'finished versus still going on'. The present perfect very often describes completed actions ("I've sent the invoice"); what decides the tense is whether the time is finished and stated (past simple) or the action is connected to now — a present result, an unstated time, or a period up to now (present perfect).

**#368 — Reference codes and links**
- Original: “both already introduced in Level 1 (L1-M1-L02), to full professional writing”
- Replacement: “both already introduced in Level 1 (Lesson 1.2), to full professional writing”
- Reason: Internal lesson code replaced.

**#369 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “/(<h2>Grammar in Context: Present Perfect vs\. Simple Past in Status Writing \(GIC-0007\)</h2>\s*<p><strong>Structure:</strong></p>\s*)<ul>.*?</blockquote>/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1<ul> <li>Use the <strong>past simple</strong> for an action at a finished, stated (or clearly understood) time: a date, a day, "last week", "yesterday", "in Q2". Never combine the present perfect with these expressions.</li> <li>Use the <strong>present perfect</strong> when the action is connected to now: a completed action whose result matters now ("I've sent the invoice"), an action at an unstated time up to now ("We've completed three audits this year"), or a situation that continues up to now (with <em>so far, since, for, yet, already</em>). This is why a report's opening summary — where things stand <em>as of today</em> — often uses it.</li> <li>In American English the past simple is also common with <em>just, already</em> and <em>yet</em> ("I already sent it"); in a formal report, the present perfect is the safer choice.</li> </ul> <blockquote> <p><strong>Formula:</strong> <em>[finished, stated time]</em> → past simple. <em>[connected to now: present result / unstated time / up to now]</em> → have/has + past participle.</p> </blockquote>”
- Reason: The lesson reduced the present perfect to 'finished versus still going on'. The present perfect very often describes completed actions ("I've sent the invoice"); what decides the tense is whether the time is finished and stated (past simple) or the action is connected to now — a present result, an unstated time, or a period up to now (present perfect).

**#370 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “"So far" signals an unfinished, ongoing situation, so it needs present perfect”
- Replacement: “"So far" connects the result to now, so it needs present perfect”
- Reason: The lesson reduced the present perfect to 'finished versus still going on'. The present perfect very often describes completed actions ("I've sent the invoice"); what decides the tense is whether the time is finished and stated (past simple) or the action is connected to now — a present result, an unstated time, or a period up to now (present perfect).

**#371 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “/(<h2>Common Mistake \(MIS-0119\)</h2>\s*).*?(?=<h2>)/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1<p><strong>Mistake:</strong> Using the present perfect with a finished-time expression (<em>have finished … last Tuesday</em>), or using the past simple with an expression that runs up to now (<em>since then, so far</em>).</p> <p>❌ "We have completed the client handover last week, and since then the new team used the system every day."</p> <p>✅ "We completed the client handover last week, and since then the new team has used the system every day."</p> <p><strong>Why it matters:</strong> A reader uses your tense choice to place events in time: closed and dated, or connected to now. A mismatch makes the timeline harder to follow — and "have completed … last week" is simply ungrammatical in standard English.</p> ”
- Reason: The original wrong example also marked a correct clause ('the new team started using the system') as an error; the new pair shows two genuine errors.

**#372 — Bengali / Hindi support**
- Original: “/(<h2>Professional Tone</h2>\s*<blockquote>\s*<p>)Bengali/Hindi note:.*?(</p>\s*</blockquote>)/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1Bengali/Hindi note: Bengali and Hindi both have perfect forms (করেছি, किया है), but they combine freely with a finished time: "আমি গতকাল পাঠিয়েছি" and "मैंने कल भेज दिया है" are natural. Translated directly, they give "I have sent it yesterday", which is wrong in English. Before writing a report sentence, ask: is there a finished time in this sentence (yesterday, last week, on Monday)? If yes, use the past simple.\2”
- Reason: The note said the L1s simply don't distinguish the two; the precise transfer problem is that Bengali and Hindi perfect forms combine with finished-time words.

- Replacement: “Only "have completed" is wrong: it is paired with "last week", a finished time, so it needs the past simple — "We completed the client handover last week." "The new team started using the system" is correct: starting is a single past event. (If you want to stress that they are still using it, you could add "and has used it every day since", but that is an addition, not a correction.)”
- Reason: The key marked a correct past simple ('started') as an error.

- Replacement: “"I sent the invoice yesterday, but the client has not confirmed receipt yet." (Also note "didn't confirmed" → after did/didn't the verb is the base form; in American English "the client didn't confirm receipt yet" is also common.)”
- Reason: Added the second error in the sentence and the American English variant.

- Replacement: “Sample: "We restocked the minibars on Monday." / "We have restocked 40 of the 60 minibars so far." Check: the past simple sentence has a finished time; the present perfect sentence has no finished time and is connected to now.”

## Level 3 · M07 Professional Grammar in Context · 7.3 Passive Voice and Reported Speech in Professional Documents (`CE-L03-M07-L03`)

**#378 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “/(<h2>Key Workplace Lesson</h2>\s*).*?(?=<div class=\"dialogue-box\">)/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1<p><strong>Passive voice</strong> — putting the thing affected first, and leaving out or moving the person who acted — isn't weak or evasive writing. In a report, it is often the more professional choice, because it keeps the focus on the event and its impact, especially before responsibility has been established. "Hasan delayed the shipment" names a person, which in an incident report can read as an accusation; "The shipment was delayed" reports the same event without naming anyone.</p> <p>Notice what the passive does <em>not</em> do: it doesn't change the facts. "The shipment was delayed due to a supplier issue" is not the passive of "Hasan delayed the shipment" — it states a different cause. Only add a cause you have confirmed.</p> <p><strong>Reported speech</strong> solves a different problem: summarizing what someone said, in writing, after the fact. With a past reporting verb (<em>said, told, mentioned</em>), the tense often moves one step back (a "backshift"): "I need it" → "he said he needed it". The backshift is not compulsory: if what was said is still true or still in the future, you can keep the original tense ("he said he needs it by Friday"). But time words and pronouns must always fit the moment you are writing.</p> ”
- Reason: Changing 'Hasan delayed the shipment' to 'The shipment was delayed due to a supplier issue' changes the facts (it names a different cause), not only the voice. The passive of the sentence is 'The shipment was delayed (by Hasan)'; a cause may be added only if it has been confirmed. Backshift is not compulsory: when the reported situation is still true or still in the future, keeping the original tense is correct ('Dr. Rahman said he needs the numbers by Friday', said on Wednesday). What must change are references that no longer fit, such as 'tomorrow' reported days later.

**#379 — Grammar / usage explanation** · source: QA review of the reference PDF pp.479–480
- Original: “"The shipment was delayed due to a supplier issue." And when Dr. Rahman called about it”
- Replacement: “"The shipment was delayed by two days." I'll add the cause once it's confirmed. And when Dr. Rahman called about it”
- Reason: Changing 'Hasan delayed the shipment' to 'The shipment was delayed due to a supplier issue' changes the facts (it names a different cause), not only the voice. The passive of the sentence is 'The shipment was delayed (by Hasan)'; a cause may be added only if it has been confirmed.

**#380 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “/(<h2>Grammar in Context: Passive Voice for Objective Reporting \(GIC-0010\)</h2>\s*).*?(?=<h2>Grammar in Context: Reported Speech)/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1<p><strong>Structure:</strong> [Thing affected] + <em>was/were</em> + [past participle] (+ <em>by</em> + agent, only if the agent genuinely matters).</p> <p><strong>Worked example:</strong></p> <p>Active: "Hasan delayed the shipment." → Passive: "The shipment was delayed." (or "…was delayed by Hasan" if the person matters)</p> <p>The facts stay the same; only the focus changes. If you know the cause, add it as a separate, checkable fact: "The shipment was delayed because the customs paperwork arrived late."</p> <p>Use passive voice when the event or its result is what the reader needs to know, and naming who did it either isn't relevant yet or isn't the point of the report. Use active voice when the person genuinely matters — for example, when recommending who should fix it.</p> ”
- Reason: Changing 'Hasan delayed the shipment' to 'The shipment was delayed due to a supplier issue' changes the facts (it names a different cause), not only the voice. The passive of the sentence is 'The shipment was delayed (by Hasan)'; a cause may be added only if it has been confirmed.

**#381 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “/(<h2>Grammar in Context: Reported Speech Tense Shifts \(GIC-0011\)</h2>\s*).*?(?=<h2>Common Mistake \(MIS-0121\)</h2>)/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1<p><strong>Structure:</strong> [Reporting verb: said / told / mentioned] + (that) + [subject] + [verb — usually backshifted one step].</p> <p><strong>Worked example:</strong></p> <p>Direct speech (Monday): Dr. Rahman said, "I need the numbers by Friday."</p> <p>Reported on Wednesday: "Dr. Rahman said (that) he needed / needs the numbers by Friday." Both are correct: Friday is still ahead, so the present tense is also possible.</p> <p>Reported the following week: "Dr. Rahman said that he needed the numbers by that Friday." The deadline has passed, so the backshift is needed.</p> <p><strong>Common backshifts:</strong> need → needed, will → would, can → could, is/are → was/were, have/has → had. <strong>Time words:</strong> today → that day, tomorrow → the next day, yesterday → the day before.</p> ”
- Reason: Backshift is not compulsory: when the reported situation is still true or still in the future, keeping the original tense is correct ('Dr. Rahman said he needs the numbers by Friday', said on Wednesday). What must change are references that no longer fit, such as 'tomorrow' reported days later.

**#382 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “/(<h2>Common Mistake \(MIS-0121\)</h2>\s*).*?(?=<h2>)/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1<p><strong>Mistake:</strong> Copying the original speaker's time words into a report written later, so they now point to the wrong day.</p> <p>❌ (Written on Friday, about something Priyanka said on Monday) "Priyanka said she can finish the draft tomorrow."</p> <p>✅ "Priyanka said (on Monday) that she could finish the draft the next day."</p> <p><strong>Why it matters:</strong> "Tomorrow" in a report written on Friday means Saturday — the reader gets the wrong date. Backshift the tense when the situation is over, and always update time words to fit the moment you are writing. (If you are reporting on the same day, "She said she can finish it tomorrow" is perfectly correct.)</p> ”
- Reason: The original MIS-0121 marked a correct sentence ('He said he needs the numbers by Friday', with Friday still ahead) as wrong. Backshift is not compulsory: when the reported situation is still true or still in the future, keeping the original tense is correct ('Dr. Rahman said he needs the numbers by Friday', said on Wednesday). What must change are references that no longer fit, such as 'tomorrow' reported days later.

- Reason: Internal lesson code replaced.

It does not change the facts — if the cause is known, add it as a separate, checkable statement.”

What must change are references that no longer fit, such as 'tomorrow' reported days later.

## Level 3 · M07 Professional Grammar in Context · 7.4 Articles, Prepositions, and Subject-Verb Agreement in Practice (`CE-L03-M07-L04`)

**#388 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “/(<h2>Grammar in Context: Fixed Preposition Pairings \(GIC-0013\)</h2>\s*).*?(?=<h2>Grammar in Context: Subject-Verb)/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1<p><strong>Structure:</strong> Some pairings are fixed — learn them as one unit: <em>responsible for, depend on, consist of, comply with, apologize (to someone) for (something)</em>.</p> <p>Others change with the meaning, so learn the meaning with the preposition:</p> <ul> <li><strong>arrive at</strong> a building or point (<em>arrive at the hotel, at the airport, at a decision</em>); <strong>arrive in</strong> a city or country (<em>arrive in Dhaka, in Delhi</em>). Never "arrive to".</li> <li><strong>agree with</strong> a person or an opinion (<em>I agree with Ms. Noor</em>); <strong>agree on</strong> a decision or point you settle together (<em>we agreed on the launch date</em>); <strong>agree to</strong> a proposal or request, or + verb (<em>she agreed to the new rate; he agreed to help</em>).</li> </ul> <p>❌ "I'm responsible of the inventory." → ✅ "I'm responsible for the inventory."</p> <p>❌ "The guests arrived to Dhaka at 6." → ✅ "The guests arrived in Dhaka at 6."</p> ”
- Reason: The lesson listed 'arrive at' and 'agree with' as fixed pairs; the preposition depends on meaning (arrive at/in; agree with/on/to).

## Level 3 · M07 Professional Grammar in Context · 7.5 Relative Clauses, Gerunds, and Infinitives for Fluent Writing (`CE-L03-M07-L05`)

**#394 — Grammar / usage explanation**
- Original: “/<p>Use <em>who</em> for people,.*?</p>/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “<p><strong>Two kinds of relative clause:</strong></p> <ul> <li><strong>Defining</strong> (no commas) — tells you <em>which</em> person or thing: "The guest <strong>who</strong> called this morning…", "The complaint <strong>that</strong> came in on Monday…". Use <em>who</em> (or <em>that</em>) for people and <em>that</em> or <em>which</em> for things; <em>that</em> is more common, especially in American English. When the pronoun is the object, you can leave it out: "the report (that) you sent".</li> <li><strong>Non-defining</strong> (with commas) — adds extra information about something already identified: "Mr. Chen, <strong>who</strong> booked a river-view room, …", "Our new booking system, <strong>which</strong> went live in May, …". Use <em>who</em> or <em>which</em> — never <em>that</em>.</li> </ul> <p><em>Which</em> can also refer back to a whole idea: "The delivery was late, which upset the guest."</p>”
- Reason: The original rule ('which with a comma for things, that without a comma') was incomplete: which also introduces defining clauses, that can refer to people, the pronoun can be omitted, and that is never used in non-defining clauses.

**#395 — Grammar / usage explanation**

- Replacement: “"We suggest reviewing the contract before signing. The client sent an email that (or which) asked for a refund." (Even shorter: "…an email asking for a refund.")”
- Reason: Added the acceptable alternatives, consistent with the corrected rule.

- Replacement: “We'll loo‿kin‿to‿(w)it (the final /k/ of "look" slides onto "into", and a light /w/ sound joins "into" to "it", because one word ends and the next begins with a vowel)”

**#418 — Pronunciation / word stress**
- Original: “(sounds like "wery," not a real word, or drifts toward "very"/"wary" ambiguity)”
- Replacement: “("wery" is not a word, so the listener has to guess; with pairs such as vest/west or vine/wine, the mix-up produces a different real word)”
- Reason: The explanation was muddled ('drifts toward very/wary ambiguity').

**#419 — Bengali / Hindi support**
- Original: “/(<blockquote>\s*<p>)Bengali/Hindi note: Bengali does not treat.*?(</p>\s*</blockquote>)/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1Bengali/Hindi note: Bengali has no separate /v/ and /w/ sounds, and Hindi व is used for both, so "very" and "wary", "vest" and "west" can sound the same. With /s/ and /sh/, the pattern differs: many Bengali speakers pronounce স, শ and ষ all as /ʃ/, so "see" can come out as "she", while some Hindi speakers do the reverse ("she" → "see"). These are not errors in Bengali or Hindi — they are different sound systems. In English these pairs distinguish real words, so a few minutes of targeted practice on exactly these pairs goes further than general pronunciation drilling.\2”
- Reason: The note was vague ('narrow the gap … in certain positions'); it now names the specific patterns: Bengali s→sh, Hindi sh→s for some speakers, and the v/w merger.

- Original: “/<h2>Scoring rubric</h2>.*?</table>/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “<h2>Scoring</h2> <p><strong>Part A (items 1–9):</strong> item 5 is multiple choice — 1 point if correct. Every other Part A item is scored 0–3, one point for each criterion below that it meets (8 items × 3 = 24).</p> <p><strong>Part B (item 10):</strong> 0–6 — one point for each of Module 7's six grammar areas corrected without introducing new errors.</p> <p><strong>Part C (items 11–12):</strong> the email and the spoken update are each scored 0–3 (6 points).</p> <table> <thead> <tr><th>Criterion (1 point)</th><th>Written items</th><th>Spoken items</th></tr> </thead> <tbody> <tr><td>Task and structure</td><td>Follows the taught structure (email anatomy, the Facts → Evidence → Analysis → Action → Recommendation framework, a specific recommendation) and answers exactly what was asked.</td><td>Follows the taught arc (meeting opening and action point, answer-or-defer, welcome and open question, filler-free delivery).</td></tr> <tr><td>Language</td><td>Level 3 vocabulary and grammar used accurately; no contractions or fragments where formality requires.</td><td>Level 3 phrases used accurately.</td></tr> <tr><td>Tone and delivery</td><td>Register fits the reader (client, manager, colleague); diplomatic where needed.</td><td>Register fits the listener; clear stress and falling intonation on statements, without filler words.</td></tr> </tbody> </table> <p>Maximum: Part A 25 + Part B 6 + Part C 6 = <strong>37 points</strong>.</p>”
- Reason: As in Levels 1–2: one weighted rubric (including pronunciation) was applied to written and spoken items alike, the explanation had typos ('Parts A's role-play items'), and no pass mark was given.

## Level 4 · — · Assessment Level 4 Assessment (`CE-L04-ASSESSMENT`)

**#557 — Assessment instructions and scoring**
- Original: “/<h2>Scoring rubric</h2>.*?</table>/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “<h2>Scoring</h2> <p>Each part is scored on four criteria, 0–3 each (0 = not shown, 1 = partly, 2 = mostly, 3 = fully), so each part is worth 12 points. Watch or read back each performance once in full before scoring; judge the performance as a whole, not individual phrases.</p> <table> <thead> <tr><th>Criterion (0–3)</th><th>Parts A and B (spoken)</th><th>Part C (written)</th></tr> </thead> <tbody> <tr><td>Structure and completeness</td><td>The stages the task calls for are all there, in order. Part A: preparation showing in the opening, an anchor, a conditional trade, the pressure tactic handled, a close that restates every term (Option 2: the pattern named, a firm line, the colleague heard, an agreed change). Part B: claim, evidence, strongest point placed deliberately and a shared-goal close (Option 2: the issues and the core question, two options with trade-offs, a decision with its reasoning).</td><td>States what is known and what is not yet known, what is already being done, and a specific time for the next update.</td></tr> <tr><td>Strategic judgment</td><td>Reads the real issue or the real doubt (not just the surface complaint or the first objection), and the response fits it; walk-away point and must-have are protected.</td><td>Accurate and honest: no false reassurance, no alarming over-statement, no guessed fix time.</td></tr> <tr><td>Composure under pressure</td><td>Stays clear and professional through the pushback, pressure tactic or doubt, without freezing, over-apologising or becoming defensive.</td><td>Calm, steady tone that a worried reader would find reassuring because it is factual.</td></tr> <tr><td>Tone, register and clarity</td><td>Firm without being harsh, warm without becoming vague; organised from the first sentence, without rambling or heavy hedging.</td><td>Register fits department heads and an external-facing list; short, plain sentences; a clear subject line.</td></tr> </tbody> </table> <p>Maximum: Part A 12 + Part B 12 + Part C 12 = <strong>36 points</strong>.</p>”

## Level 5 · M03 Coaching Through Questions · 3.1 The Difference Between Telling and Coaching (`CE-L05-M03-L01`)

**#632 — Bengali / Hindi support**
- Original: “/(<h2>Bengali/Hindi support</h2>\s*)<p><span class="bn">বাংলা</span>/<span class="bn">.*?</span></p>/ (1×)”
- Replacement: “\1<p>In many Bengali- and Hindi-speaking workplaces, a senior person who answers a question directly is seen as more respectful and helpful than one who asks a question back — so learning to coach is a real cultural adjustment, not just a technique.<br><span class="bn">অনেক বাংলাভাষী কর্মক্ষেত্রে সরাসরি উত্তর দেওয়াকে বেশি সম্মানজনক ও সহায়ক মনে করা হয় — তাই কোচিং শেখা একটি সত্যিকারের সাংস্কৃতিক মানিয়ে নেওয়া, শুধু একটি কৌশল নয়।</span><br><span class="hi">कई हिंदीभाषी कार्यस्थलों में सीधा जवाब देना ज़्यादा सम्मानजनक और मददगार माना जाता है — इसलिए कोचिंग सीखना एक वास्तविक सांस्कृतिक समायोजन है, न कि केवल एक तकनीक।</span></p>”
- Reason: The note had no English version, its language tags were misplaced (the word 'বাংলা' tagged alone, Bengali text inside the Hindi tag's sentence), and the Bengali sentence said 'the instinct to coach is a real adjustment' rather than 'learning to coach is a cultural adjustment'.

## Global rules (whole book)

**#816 — Story continuity**
- Original: “pattern /Mr\. Kabir Hossain/”
- Replacement: “Mr. Tariq Mostafa”
- Reason: The linen supplier's account manager was called 'Mr. Kabir Hossain' — the same surname as Mr. Hossain, Arif's supervisor in Level 1, and the same name as Mr. Kabir, the hotel's director (Level 1) and Head of Finance (Level 4). He is renamed Mr. Tariq Mostafa. (3 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#817 — Story continuity**
- Original: “pattern /\bHossain\b/”
- Replacement: “Mostafa”
- Reason: The linen supplier's account manager was called 'Mr. Kabir Hossain' — the same surname as Mr. Hossain, Arif's supervisor in Level 1, and the same name as Mr. Kabir, the hotel's director (Level 1) and Head of Finance (Level 4). He is renamed Mr. Tariq Mostafa. (37 replacements)


## Level 4 · M02 Difficult Conversations & Conflict Management · 2.1 Saying No Firmly and Professionally (`CE-L04-M02-L01`)

**#818 — Story continuity**
- Original: “/\bHossain\b/ (14×)”
- Replacement: “Talukder”
- Reason: A loyalty guest was also called 'Mr. Delwar Hossain', a third Hossain in the book (after Arif's supervisor and the vendor). He is renamed Mr. Delwar Talukder.


## Level 2 · M03 Telephone English Basics · 3.2 Taking a Message Accurately (`CE-L02-M03-L02`)

**#819 — Story continuity**
- Original: “/Riverside Linens/ (3×)”
- Replacement: “Delta Linens”
- Reason: Arif's own hotel is Riverside Hotel (Level 5), so other businesses can't share the name; renamed to avoid confusion.

**#821 — Story continuity**
- Original: “/\bRiverside(?='s stock\| covers\| for )/ (10×)”

## Global rules (whole book)

**#822 — Exercise and answer key alignment**
- Original: “pattern /<strong><em>(.*?)</em></strong>/”
- Replacement: “___\1___”
- Reason: Paired '___' blanks in fill-in scaffolds were converted to bold-italic markup, so the blanks disappeared and the sentence ran together (e.g. "I'm responsible for . I report to ."). (5 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#823 — Production language / unfinished material**
- Original: “pattern /\s*·\s*theme:[^<]*/”
- Replacement: “”
- Reason: Internal content-planning tag ('theme: …') printed after the motivation quote. (58 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#824 — Typography and consistency**
- Original: “pattern /<li><strong>(hotel\|healthcare\|banking\|retail):</strong>/”
- Replacement: “(computed)”
- Reason: Industry labels were inconsistently lower-case ('hotel:' beside 'Hotel:'). (37 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#825 — Typography and consistency**
- Original: “pattern /<li><strong>it:</strong>/”
- Replacement: “<li><strong>IT:</strong>”
- Reason: 'it:' reads as the pronoun; the industry is IT. (9 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#826 — Typography and consistency**
- Original: “pattern /\s+--\s+\|\s+--(?=\S)\|(?<=\S)--\s+/”
- Replacement: “ — ”
- Reason: Typewriter double hyphens replaced with a spaced em dash, the style already used elsewhere in the book. (4073 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#827 — Typography and consistency**
- Original: “pattern /\s*->\s*/”
- Replacement: “ → ”
- Reason: ASCII arrows ('->') replaced with the arrow character already used elsewhere in the book. (0 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#828 — Production language / unfinished material**
- Original: “pattern /<div class="dialogue-scene-label">[a-z0-9]+(?:-[a-z0-9]+)*</div>\s*/”
- Replacement: “”
- Reason: Internal scene identifiers (e.g. 'step-3', '02-request', 'senior-executive-email') were printed as scene titles. (75 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#829 — Production language / unfinished material**
- Original: “pattern /<h2>Industry Overlays</h2>\s*<p>None for this lesson[^<]*</p>\s*/”
- Replacement: “”
- Reason: Sections that existed only to say they were empty ('None for this lesson — … per the curriculum map'). How to Use This Book already explains that not every lesson uses every section. (12 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#830 — Headings and structure**
- Original: “pattern /<div class="dialogue-box">\s*<div class="dialogue-box-label">[^<]*</div>\s*<div class="dialogue-note">(.*?)</div>\s*</div>/”
- Replacement: “<p>\1</p>”
- Reason: 'Listen & Read' boxes that contained no dialogue, only a description of the lesson's worked examples, now read as an introductory paragraph. (8 replacements)

**#831 — Headings and structure**
- Original: “pattern /<h2>Professional Tone</h2>(\s*<blockquote>\s*<p>(?:<strong>)?Bengali/Hindi note)/”
- Replacement: “<h2>Bengali/Hindi support</h2>\1”
- Reason: Sections headed 'Professional Tone' contained only a Bengali/Hindi note, so the heading misdescribed the content. (8 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#832 — Typography and consistency**
- Original: “pattern /\s*-&gt;\s*/”
- Replacement: “ → ”
- Reason: HTML-escaped ASCII arrows ('-&gt;') replaced with the arrow character. (43 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#833 — Typography and consistency**
- Original: “pattern /\.;\s/”
- Replacement: “; ”
- Reason: Marking-criteria lists were joined with '.;' (a full stop followed by a semicolon). (14 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#834 — Typography and consistency**
- Original: “pattern /\[(Later\|A short while later)\]/”
- Replacement: “(\1)”
- Reason: Stage directions use parentheses everywhere else in the book's dialogues. (3 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#835 — Headings and structure**
- Original: “pattern /(<div class="dialogue-note">)(.*?)(</div>)/”
- Replacement: “(computed)”
- Reason: Emails and messages shown inside dialogue boxes kept their blank-line paragraph breaks only in the source; browsers collapsed them, so subject line, greeting, body and sign-off ran together on one line. (39 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#836 — Headings and structure**
- Original: “pattern /(<blockquote>)(.*?)(</blockquote>)/”
- Replacement: “(computed)”
- Reason: Model emails, agendas and minutes kept their line structure (list items, labelled lines, sign-offs) only as source line breaks, which browsers collapse — so 'Agenda: 1. … 2. … 3. …' and 'Thanks, Arif' ran together on one line. (342 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

- Reason: Production language ('continuity scenario') in industry-overlay labels. (4 replacements)

- Original: “pattern /<span class="hi">।</span>/”
- Replacement: “।”
- Reason: The Bengali full stop (danda, ।) was tagged as Hindi in the middle of Bengali sentences; it is shared punctuation and now takes the surrounding language. (18 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#847 — Typography and consistency** · source: PDF proof, batch 1 · editorial review
- Original: “pattern /(?<![\w:/.\-–])(\d{1,3})-(\d{1,3})(?![\w\-–])/”
- Replacement: “\1–\2”
- Reason: Number ranges were written with a hyphen in some places (2-3 sentences) and an en dash in others (0–4); ranges now use the en dash throughout. (68 replacements)


## Global rules (whole book)

**#848 — Typography and consistency** · source: PDF proof, batch 1 · editorial review
- Original: “pattern /\b((?:19\|20)\d\d)-((?:19\|20)\d\d)\b/”
- Replacement: “\1–\2”
- Reason: Year ranges take an en dash. (3 replacements)
